6  Matrix Effects and Commutability

This chapter describes four commutability paths implemented by commutability(): CLSI EP14-A3, CLSI EP30-A, IFCC Part 2, and IFCC Part 3. The paths answer different questions and have different statistical units.

Path Main question Unit of evaluation Main result
CLSI EP14-A3 Do processed samples follow the inter-procedure relationship of clinical samples? Procedure pair × reference material Deming prediction interval and classification
CLSI EP30-A Is a reference material suitable for a set of measurement procedures? Procedure pair × reference material Prediction interval, relative residual, and RMF comparison
IFCC Part 2 Is the difference between reference-material and clinical-sample bias acceptable? Procedure pair × reference material Bias difference, uncertainty, and three-way conclusion
IFCC Part 3 Can recalibration of a reference material reduce inter-procedure differences? Procedure set Bias before/after calibration, IMPBR, diagnostics
ImportantScope of Use

Commutability is a study-design and interpretation problem, not only a regression calculation. The package checks data structure and produces statistical boundaries, but it does not choose materials, reference procedures, clinical acceptability limits, or the final fitness-for-purpose conclusion.

Code 
library(ivdtools)
library(readr)

6.1 Main Function and Input Data

Function Main purpose
commutability() Evaluate EP14, EP30, IFCC Part 2, or IFCC Part 3 commutability
print() Summarize path, procedure pairs, design checks, and main conclusions
summary() Expand unmet design recommendations and unavailable procedure pairs
plot() Plot clinical-sample relationships, material positions, and boundaries
Field Meaning
sample_id Clinical-sample or reference-material identifier
material_type Material class; not required for the calibration path
procedure Measurement-procedure name
result Single measurement result
position Position of the material in the IFCC Part 2 run sequence
calibration_stage before or after for the calibration path

The data must preserve replicate structure, procedure labels, material class, and any run-order information required by the selected path. Do not collapse repeated results without first defining the analysis unit.

6.2 Statistical Paths

For EP14-A3, clinical-sample relationships are fitted with Deming regression and material results are checked against prediction intervals. EP30-A additionally compares relative residuals with reference-material limits. IFCC Part 2 estimates clinical-sample bias around each material and combines bias differences with uncertainty. The supported local-bias models are:

Model Interpretation
constant_global One clinical-sample bias estimate across the full concentration range
constant_local A local constant bias estimate near each reference material
linear_local A local linear bias estimated from clinical samples on both sides of the material

IFCC Part 3 evaluates calibration validity and compares inter-procedure bias before and after recalibration. Analyze original and log scales separately when the protocol requires it; an apparent change in commutability after transformation is an analysis-scale difference, not an automatic biological conclusion.

6.3 Analysis Examples

6.3.1 EP14-A3 Cholesterol on the Original Scale

Code 
ep14_c1 <- as.data.frame(read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ep14-appendix-c1.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
))
ep14_fit <- commutability(
  ep14_c1, approach = "ep14", sample_id = "sample_id",
  material_type = "material_type", procedure = "procedure", result = "result"
)
print(ep14_fit)

6.3.2 EP30 Multi-Procedure Reference Materials

Code 
ep30 <- as.data.frame(read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ep30-appendix-b.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
))
ep30_fit <- commutability(
  ep30, approach = "ep30", sample_id = "specimen",
  material_type = "type", procedure = "method", result = "result"
)
summary(ep30_fit)

6.3.3 IFCC Part 2 Bias Difference and Uncertainty

Code 
ifcc2 <- as.data.frame(read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ifcc-part2-example.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
))
ifcc2_fit <- commutability(
  ifcc2, approach = "ifcc", sample_id = "sample_id",
  material_type = "material_type", procedure = "procedure", result = "result",
  position = "position", scale = "ln",
  procedure_pairs = list(c("MPx", "MPy")), criterion = 0.12,
  bias_model = "constant_global", coverage_factor = 1.9,
  rm_position = "pooled"
)
print(ifcc2_fit)

6.3.4 IFCC Part 3 Calibration Validity

Code 
ifcc3 <- as.data.frame(read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ifcc-part3-example.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
))
ifcc3_fit <- commutability(
  ifcc3, approach = "calibration", sample_id = "sample_id",
  procedure = "procedure", result = "result",
  calibration_stage = "calibration_stage", criterion = 6
)
plot(ifcc3_fit)

6.4 Interpretation

Prediction-interval classification and bias-difference classification need not agree. A reference material can lie within an EP14/EP30 prediction interval yet have an unacceptable bias difference under IFCC Part 2, or vice versa, because the paths target different estimands and uncertainty models. The final report should state the path, scale, procedure pair, material definition, repeated-measurement handling, limit, and any procedure pairs that were unavailable or failed design checks.

6.5 Detailed Standard Cases

6.5.1 CLSI EP14-A3 Appendix C Example C1: Cholesterol on the Original Scale

Appendix C Example C1 contains 20 patient samples and 5 processed samples. Both procedures have three replicate measurements for cholesterol in mg/dL. Table C1 labels the last two processed samples as “d”; the data file names them d_run16 and d_run07 according to run order only to keep material identities unique. The measurement values are unchanged.

Code 
ep14_c1_data <- read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ep14-appendix-c1.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
)
head(ep14_c1_data, 6)

6.5.2 CLSI EP14-A3 Appendix C Example C2: Myoglobin on the log10 Scale

Example C2 contains 20 patient samples and 7 processed samples for myoglobin in ng/mL. Because the dispersion of differences on the original scale increases with concentration, the standard applies a log10 transformation to every replicate result before fitting the Deming relationship.

)

6.5.3 CLSI EP30-A Appendix B: Multi-Procedure Reference-Material Data

EP30-A Appendix B provides 25 clinical samples, 7 candidate reference materials, 10 measurement procedures, and three replicate measurements per combination.

Code 
ep30_raw <- read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ep30-appendix-b.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
)

6.5.3.1 MA–MB and MA–MD Regression Results

Use approach = "ep30" directly. The seven candidate reference materials are used independently in this example, so rm_set_size = 1; the function does not automatically treat the total number of materials as the size of a correlated set.

Code 
ep30_result <- commutability(
  ep30_raw,
  sample_id = "specimen",
  material_type = "type",
  procedure = "method",
  result = "result",
  approach = "ep30",
  scale = "raw",
  procedure_pairs = list(c("MA", "MB"), c("MA", "MD")),
  conf.level = 0.95,
  rm_set_size = 1,
  relative_residual_limit = 2
)

6.5.3.2 Reference-Material Predictions and Relative Residuals

The relative residual in EP30 Table 1 is the distance from the reference material to the regression line divided by the clinical-sample regression standard error. commutability() returns the sign used in the table, predicted value − measured value, and uses \(n-1\) as the denominator degrees of freedom for \(S_{y.x}\) as in the example. Prediction intervals are calculated separately using Appendix C16–C20.

EP30-A Appendix B Table 1: regression parameters and relative residuals
标准位置 统计量 标准结果 ivdtools结果
EP30 MA-MB intercept 3.660 3.6495
EP30 MA-MB slope 0.961 0.9618
EP30 MA-MB syx 1.188 1.1890
EP30 MA-MB RMA 1.088 1.0732
EP30 MA-MB RMB -0.117 -0.0594
EP30 MA-MB RMC 0.601 0.5869
EP30 MA-MB RMD 2.164 2.1347
EP30 MA-MB RME -0.523 -0.5338
EP30 MA-MB RMF 3.241 3.2360
EP30 MA-MB RMG -0.923 -0.9248
EP30 MA-MD intercept 0.396 0.3986
EP30 MA-MD slope 0.834 0.8345
EP30 MA-MD syx 1.033 1.0324
EP30 MA-MD RMA -0.603 -0.6333
EP30 MA-MD RMB 0.518 0.5904
EP30 MA-MD RMC 0.326 0.3285
EP30 MA-MD RMD 0.997 0.9818
EP30 MA-MD RME -0.021 -0.0328
EP30 MA-MD RMF -6.758 -6.7583
EP30 MA-MD RMG -0.066 -0.1116

Under the ±2\(S_{y.x}\) rule, RMF in MA–MD is noncommutable; RMD and RMF in MA–MB exceed the limits. RMD remains commutable under the 95% prediction-interval method for MA–MB, showing that materials near a boundary can receive different classifications under different evaluation rules.

Prediction intervals and relative residuals classify RMD in MA–MB differently: the former calls it commutable, while the latter calls it noncommutable. This is a boundary difference caused by the evaluation rule; do not merge the two columns into an undefined “final classification.”

6.5.4 IFCC Part 2 Official Supplement: Bias Difference and Uncertainty

The original IFCC Part 2 workbook contains 50 clinical samples and 5 reference materials. Following the supplement’s exclusions, all results for CS21 and the third replicate of CS6 on MPx are removed, leaving 49 clinical samples. Each reference material has five positions and three replicates per procedure. The analysis uses the natural-log scale, criterion \(C=0.12\), and coverage factor \(k=1.9\).

Code 
ifcc_data <- read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ifcc-part2-example.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
)
table(ifcc_data$material_type, ifcc_data$procedure)
          
           MPx MPy
  clinical 146 147
  rm        75  75
Code 
ifcc_result <- commutability(
  ifcc_data,
  sample_id = "sample_id",
  material_type = "material_type",
  procedure = "procedure",
  result = "result",
  position = "position",
  approach = "ifcc",
  scale = "ln",
  procedure_pairs = list(c("MPx", "MPy")),
  criterion = 0.12,
  bias_model = "constant_global",
  coverage_factor = 1.9,
  rm_position = "pooled"
)
ifcc_result

Code 
plot(ifcc_result)

The classification of RM3 depends on pooling the position-mean variance across reference materials as specified by the official method. If position variance is estimated separately for each material, both the algorithm and classification definition change; formal reports must record the rm_position setting.

6.5.5 IFCC Part 3 Official Supplement: Calibration-Validity Assessment

The official IFCC Part 3 case contains 40 clinical samples and 7 measurement procedures. Table S1 contains results from the manufacturer’s original calibration scheme. For each clinical sample, the initial target is the mean of the middle five values after removing the largest and smallest of the seven procedure results. Table S2 contains results after recalibration with two candidate reference-material levels but still uses the initial targets from Table S1; targets must not be redefined from the post-calibration results.

Code 
ifcc_part3_data <- read_csv(
  "./data/006-基质效应与互换性/std-ifcc-part3-example.csv",
  show_col_types = FALSE
)
table(
  ifcc_part3_data$calibration_stage,
  ifcc_part3_data$procedure
)
        
         MP1 MP2 MP3 MP4 MP5 MP6 MP7
  after   40  40  40  40  40  40  40
  before  40  40  40  40  40  40  40

First evaluate all seven procedures. criterion = 6 is the prespecified IMPBR percentage criterion in the paper example; w3_reference_procedure = "MP1" applies the MP1 SD/W(3) ratio to adjust each procedure’s W(3), as in the original analysis.

Code 
ifcc_part3_result <- commutability(
  ifcc_part3_data,
  sample_id = "sample_id",
  procedure = "procedure",
  result = "result",
  approach = "calibration",
  calibration_stage = "calibration_stage",
  criterion = 6,
  w3_reference_procedure = "MP1"
)

ifcc_part3_excluding_mp6 <- commutability(
  ifcc_part3_data,
  sample_id = "sample_id",
  procedure = "procedure",
  result = "result",
  approach = "calibration",
  calibration_stage = "calibration_stage",
  criterion = 6,
  exclude_procedures = "MP6",
  w3_reference_procedure = "MP1"
)

IFCC Part 3 / after / EXCLUDE_MP6 impbr_percent 1.50 1.3976

For the complete procedure set, IMPBR decreases from approximately 76.6% before calibration to approximately 21.6% after calibration, but remains above 6%, so the function returns “requires investigation.” It does not automatically classify the candidate reference material as noncommutable for all procedures or remove a procedure. The original investigation identified the candidate reference material as unsuitable for MP6; after explicitly setting exclude_procedures = "MP6", the IMPBR for the remaining six procedures is approximately 1.4% based on the values listed in the supplement, meeting the 6% criterion.

The paper reports an IMPBR of 1.5% after excluding MP6, whereas recalculation from the published Table S1 and S2 values gives approximately 1.4%. The unrounded internal values used in the paper were not published with the supplement, so it is not possible to determine whether the approximately 0.1 percentage-point difference is caused only by rounding; both values are retained as reported.

Code 
plot(ifcc_part3_result)

leave_one_out shows sensitivity results after omitting each procedure and is intended only to identify an investigation direction. Formal conclusions require support from the calibration model, precision, and interference investigations; the MP6 exclusion in this case is the conclusion of the original investigation, not a procedure selected automatically by the software to minimize IMPBR.

6.5.6 Real-Application Comparison: POCT Glucose EQA Materials

Wang et al. reported a real-application study in 2020: three EQA materials at three concentration levels were measured together with 33 individual clinical samples on five POCT glucose meters and one Hitachi 7600 analyzer. The study used both the CLSI EP30-A Deming-regression 95% prediction interval and the IFCC bias-difference method. After exclusions, the IFCC analysis used 30 clinical samples, a 10% criterion, and a coverage factor of 1.9.

EQA material CLSI prediction-interval result IFCC bias-difference result
In-house human whole blood (HWB) All three levels were commutable on all 5/5 POCT meters The three levels were commutable on 3/5, 4/5, and 3/5 meters; two meters were commutable at all three levels
Commercial control material (CCM) The three levels were commutable on 3/5, 2/5, and 4/5 meters All 5 meters were inconclusive or noncommutable
Pooled human serum (PHS) The three levels were commutable on 4/5, 3/5, and 5/5 meters All 5 meters were inconclusive or noncommutable

Both paths showed that in-house human whole blood performed better than commercial control material and pooled human serum, while the IFCC conclusion was more conservative. The investigators suggested that inconclusive results could be related to insufficient replicate or clinical-sample counts, poorer measurement precision, and larger sample-specific differences. This case illustrates that prediction intervals and bias differences address different questions and handle uncertainty differently; the same material can receive different classifications for different procedure pairs, concentrations, and decision paths.

The paper does not provide enough individual measurements to reconstruct the complete numerical analysis here. This is therefore a real-application comparison of study design and published results, not numerical validation evidence for ivdtools. See Wang et al., 2020, Journal of Clinical Laboratory Analysis.
